========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not much. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.

Those tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.

Yeah… yeah, I think so. Folks at work keep asking me, “You alright? You sick or something?” ’Cause I’m walking around slower than usual, like I’m dragging through mud.

And it ain’t just walking. Somebody’ll ask me something simple—like where a shipment’s supposed to go or what time somebody’s coming in—and I just… take forever to get the words out. I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.

Used to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.

It’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.

Used to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.

It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. I mean, I used to be out there messing with my tomato plants, checking on them like they was children or something. And after church I’d usually stand around talking with folks, you know, catching up. Lately I just look at all that stuff like… like it belongs to somebody else. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",

  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no.

I mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.

And Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.

But lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.

About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. Just… nothing much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep going over my daughter leaving and telling myself I must have failed her somehow, like a better father would have done something different.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have.

I keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.

I know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. Like I didn’t do enough.

Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.\n\nI know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.

When I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. Like my body’s got wet cement in it. Folks at work keep asking me if I’m sick, because I’m walking slower than normal and I’ll just stand there taking forever to answer some simple question. And I know the answer, it just… takes me a minute to get it out.

I used to move pretty quick on the floor, you know, keep an eye on everything. Now it feels like I’m dragging myself around. Like moving through mud.

My supervisor noticed I wasn’t right, and I ended up telling him a little, and… well, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I guess. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. I keep thinking about her packing up, and you know, I was trying to act like it was fine, like dads are supposed to do, but… yeah, this whole change in me has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to be out there messing with my tomato plants, checking on them like they was children or something. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. But I’m not eating right. Most days I gotta make myself take a few bites, you know?\n\nEven stuff I normally like—somebody brings something good by the warehouse, or my wife fixes something at home—it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would do it if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I got that far, Doc. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s been messing things up pretty bad.

At work, I’ve made mistakes I don’t usually make, and it got serious enough that my supervisor found me sitting out in my truck before dawn. I don’t even know what I was doing exactly, just sitting there. Then they got occupational health involved, which… that’s embarrassing. I’ve been there a long time, you know, I’m usually the one keeping things straight.

And at home, too. Bills, little responsibilities, stuff I’d normally just handle—I’ve been letting it pile up. Mail on the table, things I know need doing. I keep meaning to get to it, and then I just… don’t.

My supervisor noticed I wasn’t right, and I ended up telling him a little, and… well, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I guess. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. I keep thinking about her packing up, and you know, I was trying to act like it was fine, like dads are supposed to do, but… yeah, this whole change in me has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to be out there messing with my tomato plants, checking on them like they was children or something. And I got this little bench where I sand wood—nothing fancy, just keeps my hands busy.\n\nI’d watch the Falcons, too, even when they’re breaking my heart like they always do. And after church I’d usually stand around talking with folks, you know, catching up. Lately I just look at all that stuff like… like it belongs to somebody else. I don’t really feel pulled to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure when you did happen to do those things, like watching the Falcons or sanding wood, over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not much. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. But I’m not eating right. Most days I gotta make myself take a few bites, you know?\n\nEven stuff I normally like—somebody brings something good by the warehouse, or my wife fixes something at home—it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Folks at work keep asking me, “You alright? You sick or something?” ’Cause I’m walking around slower than usual, like I’m dragging through mud.\n\nAnd it ain’t just walking. Somebody’ll ask me something simple—like where a shipment’s supposed to go or what time somebody’s coming in—and I just… take forever to get the words out. I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.\n\nUsed to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would do it if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I got that far, Doc. It’s more like I keep thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. And that thought scared me.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being the one that keeps things moving, you know, at home and at the warehouse—when a truck’s late or somebody’s mixed up an order, they come find me. So having that thought in my head… that I’d be less trouble gone… it shook me enough that I told my supervisor. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly about my daughter.\n\nI keep going back over her leaving, like I’m replaying it in my head, and I keep telling myself I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different, you know?\n\nAnd I know kids grow up, they make their own choices… my brother used to say that about his boy, too, when he moved off for that job in Tulsa—but it don’t stop me from laying there thinking, “No, this is on me.” Like I missed something I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.\n\nUsed to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently bad the whole time?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I guess. I mean, this whole change in me… it started right around when my daughter left Georgia for the military, and that’s been about two weeks now.\n\nThere might be a few minutes here and there where I’m distracted, like if somebody at the warehouse is talking about a shipment or—well, we had this one driver last year who got lost three times in the same week—but no, overall it’s been bad since she left. About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.\n\nBut lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.\n\nWhen I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. I keep thinking about her packing up, and you know, I was trying to act like it was fine, like dads are supposed to do, but… yeah, this whole change in me has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. But I’m not eating right. Most days I gotta make myself take a few bites, you know?\n\nEven stuff I normally like—somebody brings something good by the warehouse, or my wife fixes something at home—it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Folks at work keep asking me, “You alright? You sick or something?” ’Cause I’m walking around slower than usual, like I’m dragging through mud.\n\nAnd it ain’t just walking. Somebody’ll ask me something simple—like where a shipment’s supposed to go or what time somebody’s coming in—and I just… take forever to get the words out. I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.\n\nUsed to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would do it if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I got that far, Doc. So having that thought in my head… that I’d be less trouble gone… it shook me enough that I told my supervisor. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different, you know?\n\nAnd I know kids grow up, they make their own choices… my brother used to say that about his boy, too, when he moved off for that job in Tulsa—but it don’t stop me from laying there thinking, “No, this is on me.” Like I missed something I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.\n\nUsed to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently bad the whole time?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I guess. I mean, this whole change in me… it started right around when my daughter left Georgia for the military, and that’s been about two weeks now.\n\nThere might be a few minutes here and there where I’m distracted, like if somebody at the warehouse is talking about a shipment or—well, we had this one driver last year who got lost three times in the same week—but no, overall it’s been bad since she left. About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.\n\nBut lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.\n\nI know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. Like I didn’t do enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific way you would act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way, not like that.\n\nIt’s more like I keep having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I don’t usually talk like that—at work I’m the one telling everybody to keep moving, trucks don’t unload themselves, that kind of thing—but it got bad enough that I told my supervisor. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.\n\nWhen I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. Like my body’s got wet cement in it. Folks at work keep asking me if I’m sick, because I’m walking slower than normal and I’ll just stand there taking forever to answer some simple question. Like moving through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been messing things up pretty bad.\n\nAt work, I’ve made mistakes I don’t usually make, and it got serious enough that my supervisor found me sitting out in my truck before dawn. Not really, no. I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a whole day feeling normal.

I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.\n\nUsed to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. It’s more like I keep thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. And that thought scared me.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being the one that keeps things moving, you know, at home and at the warehouse—when a truck’s late or somebody’s mixed up an order, they come find me. So having that thought in my head… that I’d be less trouble gone… it shook me enough that I told my supervisor. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly about my daughter.\n\nI keep going back over her leaving, like I’m replaying it in my head, and I keep telling myself I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different, you know?\n\nAnd I know kids grow up, they make their own choices… my brother used to say that about his boy, too, when he moved off for that job in Tulsa—but it don’t stop me from laying there thinking, “No, this is on me.” Like I missed something I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.\n\nUsed to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently bad the whole time?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I guess. I mean, this whole change in me… it started right around when my daughter left Georgia for the military, and that’s been about two weeks now.\n\nThere might be a few minutes here and there where I’m distracted, like if somebody at the warehouse is talking about a shipment or—well, we had this one driver last year who got lost three times in the same week—but no, overall it’s been bad since she left. About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.\n\nBut lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.\n\nI know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. Like I didn’t do enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific way you would act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way, not like that.\n\nIt’s more like I keep having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.\n\nWhen I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. Like my body’s got wet cement in it. Folks at work keep asking me if I’m sick, because I’m walking slower than normal and I’ll just stand there taking forever to answer some simple question. And I know the answer, it just… takes me a minute to get it out.\n\nI used to move pretty quick on the floor, you know, keep an eye on everything. Now it feels like I’m dragging myself around. Like moving through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been messing things up pretty bad.\n\nAt work, I’ve made mistakes I don’t usually make, and it got serious enough that my supervisor found me sitting out in my truck before dawn. I don’t even know what I was doing exactly, just sitting there. Then they got occupational health involved, which… that’s embarrassing. I’ve been there a long time, you know, I’m usually the one keeping things straight.\n\nAnd at home, too. Bills, little responsibilities, stuff I’d normally just handle—I’ve been letting it pile up. Mail on the table, things I know need doing. I keep meaning to get to it, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s really why I came in.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan or anything—sorry, I know you didn’t ask that exactly—but it’s more this thought that keeps showing up, like maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I kept trying to push it down, like you do when there’s work to get done, but it kept coming back.\n\nI ended up telling my supervisor, which I don’t usually do with personal stuff. I’m not the type to make a scene. But it felt… serious enough that I shouldn’t keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt nearly normal, without any of these thoughts or feelings that have been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a whole day feeling normal.\n\nThis whole change in me has been going on about two weeks now, ever since my daughter left Georgia for the military. And I know kids grow up, that’s what they’re supposed to do—my daddy used to say if you raise them right, they leave—but it’s like after she went, something in me just dropped. That’s really why I came in.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan or anything—sorry, I know you didn’t ask that exactly—but it’s more this thought that keeps showing up, like maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I kept trying to push it down, like you do when there’s work to get done, but it kept coming back.\n\nI ended up telling my supervisor, which I don’t usually do with personal stuff. I’m not the type to make a scene. But it felt… serious enough that I shouldn’t keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt nearly normal, without any of these thoughts or feelings that have been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a whole day feeling normal.\n\nThis whole change in me has been going on about two weeks now, ever since my daughter left Georgia for the military. And I know kids grow up, that’s what they’re supposed to do—my daddy used to say if you raise them right, they leave—but it’s like after she went, something in me just dropped. Since then I haven’t really felt like myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt any pleasure or enjoyment from activities that you normally find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to mess with my tomato plants, sand wood at my bench, watch the Falcons, and stay after church talking, but lately I just look at all that like it belongs to somebody else.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not much, no. I mean, I used to get something out of little things… messing with my tomato plants, sanding wood out at my bench, watching the Falcons even when they make you mad. After church I’d usually hang around and talk, you know, catch up with folks.

Lately I just kind of look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but they don’t feel like they’re for me right now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I, uh, I came in because I’ve been having these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I don’t usually talk like that, you know—I’m the one at work people come to when things are backed up or something breaks down. My supervisor noticed I wasn’t right, and I ended up telling him a little, and… well, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I guess. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. I keep thinking about her packing up, and you know, I was trying to act like it was fine, like dads are supposed to do, but… yeah, this whole change in me has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to be out there messing with my tomato plants, checking on them like they was children or something. And I got this little bench where I sand wood—nothing fancy, just keeps my hands busy.\n\nI’d watch the Falcons, too, even when they’re breaking my heart like they always do. And after church I’d usually stand around talking with folks, you know, catching up. Lately I just look at all that stuff like… like it belongs to somebody else. I don’t really feel pulled to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure when you did happen to do those things, like watching the Falcons or sanding wood, over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not much. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. But I’m not eating right. Most days I gotta make myself take a few bites, you know?\n\nEven stuff I normally like—somebody brings something good by the warehouse, or my wife fixes something at home—it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Folks at work keep asking me, “You alright? You sick or something?” ’Cause I’m walking around slower than usual, like I’m dragging through mud.\n\nAnd it ain’t just walking. Somebody’ll ask me something simple—like where a shipment’s supposed to go or what time somebody’s coming in—and I just… take forever to get the words out. I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.\n\nUsed to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would do it if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I got that far, Doc. It’s more like I keep thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. And that thought scared me.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being the one that keeps things moving, you know, at home and at the warehouse—when a truck’s late or somebody’s mixed up an order, they come find me. So having that thought in my head… that I’d be less trouble gone… it shook me enough that I told my supervisor. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly about my daughter.\n\nI keep going back over her leaving, like I’m replaying it in my head, and I keep telling myself I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different, you know?\n\nAnd I know kids grow up, they make their own choices… my brother used to say that about his boy, too, when he moved off for that job in Tulsa—but it don’t stop me from laying there thinking, “No, this is on me.” Like I missed something I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.\n\nUsed to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently bad the whole time?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I guess. I mean, this whole change in me… it started right around when my daughter left Georgia for the military, and that’s been about two weeks now.\n\nThere might be a few minutes here and there where I’m distracted, like if somebody at the warehouse is talking about a shipment or—well, we had this one driver last year who got lost three times in the same week—but no, overall it’s been bad since she left. About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.\n\nBut lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.\n\nI know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. Like I didn’t do enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific way you would act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way, not like that.\n\nIt’s more like I keep having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I don’t usually talk like that—at work I’m the one telling everybody to keep moving, trucks don’t unload themselves, that kind of thing—but it got bad enough that I told my supervisor. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.\n\nWhen I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. Like my body’s got wet cement in it. Folks at work keep asking me if I’m sick, because I’m walking slower than normal and I’ll just stand there taking forever to answer some simple question. Most of the time, I’d say. I mean, I keep trying to act like I can still handle things the way I always have, but… it’s been showing up.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I, uh, I came in because I’ve been having these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I don’t usually talk like that, you know—I’m the one at work people come to when things are backed up or something breaks down. My supervisor noticed I wasn’t right, and I ended up telling him a little, and… well, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: About two weeks, I guess. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. I keep thinking about her packing up, and you know, I was trying to act like it was fine, like dads are supposed to do, but… yeah, this whole change in me has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I used to be out there messing with my tomato plants, checking on them like they was children or something. And I got this little bench where I sand wood—nothing fancy, just keeps my hands busy.\n\nI’d watch the Falcons, too, even when they’re breaking my heart like they always do. And after church I’d usually stand around talking with folks, you know, catching up. Lately I just look at all that stuff like… like it belongs to somebody else. I don’t really feel pulled to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure when you did happen to do those things, like watching the Falcons or sanding wood, over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not much. I mean, I’ll sit down like I’m gonna watch the Falcons, and then I’m just staring at the screen more than watching it. Same with the sanding bench—I’ll go out there and touch the wood, move a tool around, then just kind of stand there.\n\nThose tomato plants used to make me feel good, you know? I’d be checking them every morning, fussing over them. My wife used to laugh at me for that. But lately it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard, somebody else’s hobbies. Even after church, I don’t feel like standing around talking like I used to. It’s just… flat, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I don’t know a number. But I’m not eating right. Most days I gotta make myself take a few bites, you know?\n\nEven stuff I normally like—somebody brings something good by the warehouse, or my wife fixes something at home—it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe poke at it, and then end up throwing it out later. Funny, I used to be the one finishing everybody’s plate… but lately, no, food just don’t seem worth fooling with.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Folks at work keep asking me, “You alright? You sick or something?” ’Cause I’m walking around slower than usual, like I’m dragging through mud.\n\nAnd it ain’t just walking. Somebody’ll ask me something simple—like where a shipment’s supposed to go or what time somebody’s coming in—and I just… take forever to get the words out. I’ll be standing there looking at ’em, knowing I ought to answer quicker.\n\nUsed to, I could move around that warehouse pretty good, keep things rolling. Now it feels like my body don’t want to catch up with what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would do it if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I got that far, Doc. It’s more like I keep thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up one morning. And that thought scared me.\n\nI mean, I’m used to being the one that keeps things moving, you know, at home and at the warehouse—when a truck’s late or somebody’s mixed up an order, they come find me. So having that thought in my head… that I’d be less trouble gone… it shook me enough that I told my supervisor. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly about my daughter.\n\nI keep going back over her leaving, like I’m replaying it in my head, and I keep telling myself I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different, you know?\n\nAnd I know kids grow up, they make their own choices… my brother used to say that about his boy, too, when he moved off for that job in Tulsa—but it don’t stop me from laying there thinking, “No, this is on me.” Like I missed something I should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like I’m pacing holes in the floor or anything.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, really. People at work keep asking me if I’m sick, ’cause I’m walking around so slow. Like I’m moving through mud. Even when somebody asks me something simple, I just stand there too long before I answer.\n\nUsed to be I could keep up with everybody in that warehouse—well, most everybody, anyway. There was this one young guy, moved like he had springs in his shoes—but now I feel like my body just won’t get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently bad the whole time?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty steady, I guess. I mean, this whole change in me… it started right around when my daughter left Georgia for the military, and that’s been about two weeks now.\n\nThere might be a few minutes here and there where I’m distracted, like if somebody at the warehouse is talking about a shipment or—well, we had this one driver last year who got lost three times in the same week—but no, overall it’s been bad since she left. About two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest or excitement about anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.\n\nI mean, I used to have my little things. I’d go out and fool with my tomato plants—argue with the dirt like that was gonna make ’em grow faster. Or I’d be out at my workbench sanding a piece of wood smooth, just kind of losing time in it.\n\nAnd Sundays, I’d watch the Falcons, even when they were making me mad, which is most Sundays, if I’m being honest. After church I’d usually hang around talking, too—there’s a fella there who can turn a five-minute story into forty, and somehow I’d still stand there listening.\n\nBut lately… I look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but I don’t feel any pull toward them. No real excitement. Just… nothing much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have.\n\nI keep turning it over in my head, my daughter leaving like that, and I start thinking I must’ve failed her somehow. Like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve said the right thing or done something different before she left.\n\nI know kids grow up and make their own choices—my cousin’s boy ran off to Texas once and everybody acted like he’d joined the circus—but… I still keep landing back on it being my fault somehow. Like I didn’t do enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific way you would act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way, not like that.\n\nIt’s more like I keep having this thought that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I don’t usually talk like that—at work I’m the one telling everybody to keep moving, trucks don’t unload themselves, that kind of thing—but it got bad enough that I told my supervisor. I didn’t want to keep it just in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're fidgeting or can't sit still when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Not so much fidgeting, no… it’s kind of the opposite.\n\nWhen I’m not working—or even at work, honestly—I feel slowed down. Like my body’s got wet cement in it. Folks at work keep asking me if I’m sick, because I’m walking slower than normal and I’ll just stand there taking forever to answer some simple question. And I know the answer, it just… takes me a minute to get it out.\n\nI used to move pretty quick on the floor, you know, keep an eye on everything. Now it feels like I’m dragging myself around. Like moving through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been messing things up pretty bad.\n\nAt work, I’ve made mistakes I don’t usually make, and it got serious enough that my supervisor found me sitting out in my truck before dawn. I don’t even know what I was doing exactly, just sitting there. Then they got occupational health involved, which… that’s embarrassing. I’ve been there a long time, you know, I’m usually the one keeping things straight.\n\nAnd at home, too. Bills, little responsibilities, stuff I’d normally just handle—I’ve been letting it pile up. Mail on the table, things I know need doing. I keep meaning to get to it, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. That’s really why I came in.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan or anything—sorry, I know you didn’t ask that exactly—but it’s more this thought that keeps showing up, like maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And that scared me. I kept trying to push it down, like you do when there’s work to get done, but it kept coming back.\n\nI ended up telling my supervisor, which I don’t usually do with personal stuff. I’m not the type to make a scene. But it felt… serious enough that I shouldn’t keep it to myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past two weeks where you felt nearly normal, without any of these thoughts or feelings that have been bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe a few hours here and there where I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a whole day feeling normal.\n\nThis whole change in me has been going on about two weeks now, ever since my daughter left Georgia for the military. And I know kids grow up, that’s what they’re supposed to do—my daddy used to say if you raise them right, they leave—but it’s like after she went, something in me just dropped. Since then I haven’t really felt like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment from activities that you normally find pleasurable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I used to get something out of little things… messing with my tomato plants, sanding wood out at my bench, watching the Falcons even when they make you mad. After church I’d usually hang around and talk, you know, catch up with folks.\n\nLately I just kind of look at all that like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are my things, but they don’t feel like they’re for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that these thoughts and feelings have been interfering with your daily life most of the time or only some of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I’d say. I mean, I keep trying to act like I can still handle things the way I always have, but… it’s been showing up.\n\nAt work I made some mistakes—more than just little slip-ups—and it got bad enough that my supervisor found me sitting in my truck before dawn. I wasn’t even doing anything, just sitting there. He got occupational health involved after that, which was embarrassing. I’ve been working since I was a kid, pretty much, so having somebody step in like that… it got to me.\n\nAnd at home, too. Bills and things I’d normally take care of, I’ve just let them stack up. My wife used to say I was the one who kept the household running straight, and now I look at the mail and just set it down somewhere. So yeah, it’s not just once in a while. It’s been messing with daily life pretty regularly."
